| D76 | The forfeit deadline lives on the SEAT, and the opponent can see it count down (P4 online, increment 3c-v) | 3c-ii could forfeit an abandoned player and 3c-iv made a graceful leave do it instantly, but the one path that still WAITS — an ungraceful drop (crash, killed tab, dead network) — waited in total silence: the player still there watched a motionless board for 90 seconds and was then handed a win with no explanation of what had just been decided. **The decision is where the deadline lives.** It moved out of the DO's own `ABANDON_KEY` storage list and onto **`PlayerState.forfeitAt`** in the lobby snapshot, which is a wire type — so the number the opponent's countdown renders is literally the number the alarm fires on, and the two cannot drift. The snapshot was the right carrier rather than the `match` frame for three converging reasons: the arming moment (a disconnect grace expiring in `alarm()`) already commits a snapshot and sends no match frame; a forfeit is lobby/transport state that the engine knows nothing about; and a reconnecting client gets it for free with the state it already receives. Mirroring it into the snapshot while `ABANDON_KEY` stayed authoritative was rejected outright — two sources for one deadline is exactly how a countdown ends up promising something the server won't do. **The invariant that makes it safe is `connected` ⇒ `forfeitAt === null`**, enforced by making `setSeatConnected` take the deadline as its final argument (defaulting to null, so you cannot write one without deciding the other) and by clearing it in `seatGuest` / `reseatHost` / `makePlayer` — the three ways a seat is marked present. That REPLACED D73's `clearDisconnectTimers`, whose whole reason for existing was the fear that a future branch would cancel one timer and leave the other armed: with the deadline on the seat, "mark them present" and "don't forfeit them" became a single indivisible write instead of two calls. `forfeitAbandoned` dissolved into the alarm, which now judges due deadlines on the FINAL snapshot and consumes them in the same commit. **A second, quieter decision: `readSnapshot`.** `SNAPSHOT_KEY` has no version gate, so adding a field would have made every in-flight lobby unreadable across the deploy; the snapshot is now read through a normalizer that fills fields added since it was written. That is the OPPOSITE of D72's answer for `MatchRecord`, deliberately: a lobby snapshot is a handful of scalars with no embedded engine state, so a missing field has exactly one sensible value and the cost of being wrong is cosmetic — whereas a `GameState` from a build whose rules moved reads back "fine" and plays wrong. The guard is on the field's TYPE, not on nullishness, because it feeds `Math.min` in the alarm scheduler and an `undefined` would set the next alarm to NaN. **Client:** a top-centre `OpponentAwayBanner` — "Opp disconnected — forfeits in 1:30", ticking; "forfeiting…" past zero rather than a frozen 0:00, because the server's alarm is what actually ends it and may be a moment behind; "waiting for them to reconnect…" when no clock is armed. Hidden once the game is over. 1841 → **1853** tests, `bun run check` green. **BROWSER-VERIFIED on two real clients, all three paths:** killing a client outright brought the banner up at +5s with a full 1:30, counted it down live, and fired the forfeit at zero ("You win! / The match was forfeited.") — which is also the proof that moving the deadline did not break the scheduling that D73 nearly shipped broken; and a genuine reconnect (same tab navigated away and back, so the playerId survived) cleared the banner at once and left the match running well past the original deadline, i.e. the returning player was NOT forfeited. Zero page errors. **The run also exposed a PRE-EXISTING gap, recorded not fixed:** a HALF-OPEN socket is invisible to the server — `setWebSocketAutoResponse` answers keepalives without waking the DO and nothing watches for pings that stop arriving, so a player whose network dies without a TCP close is never marked disconnected and never forfeits. Every ordinary way of leaving (tab close, browser kill, navigation, reload, crash) does produce a close event, which is why this has never bitten; closing it needs server-side liveness (a per-socket `lastSeen` instead of the auto-response, or an idle sweep) and is its own slice. *Why: a countdown is a promise about what the server will do, so it must read the server's own number — putting the deadline on the wire type that the alarm consults is the only shape where that is true by construction rather than by discipline. And folding the cancel into the same write as "they're back" turns the scariest failure mode here — forfeiting a player who returned — from a thing you have to remember into a thing you cannot express.* (2026-07-26) |
| D77 | Rematch is a lifecycle FRAME that ends the match and readies the asker (P4 online, increment 3c-vi) | The hole 3c-iv opened on purpose: since a mid-match `bye` keeps the seat, an in-game lobby had no route back to `selecting` except the host's kick, so two players who had just finished a game were forced to mint a new code to play again. **Three shapes were on the table, and the middle one won.** A mutual OFFER/ACCEPT handshake ("waiting for them to accept…") means a second piece of lobby state to persist, expire and reconcile, and a screen that can wedge if the answer never comes. A plain `endMatch` that drops both players on the versus screen to re-ready is correct but makes the asker press twice and records nothing about who asked. The shipped shape — **`endMatch` + ready the asker**, one commit — reuses the ready-up flow VERBATIM as the agreement mechanism: the countdown still starts only when both seats are ready, so pressing Rematch *is* an offer, readying *is* the acceptance, and un-readying on the versus screen *is* the withdrawal, all through machinery that already existed and is already tested. The pure helper is literally `applyIntent(endMatch(snapshot), slot, {type: "set-ready", ready: true})` — routing through `applyIntent` rather than writing `ready: true` keeps "you can't be ready without a deck" in one place, so a player whose saved deck vanished between games lands back un-readied instead of in an impossible state. **It is a `{kind: "rematch", from}` client FRAME, not a `LobbyIntent` member**, because its legality is not a property of the snapshot the shared reducer sees: only the DO can tell whether the match it holds has actually reached `gameOver`, and a rematch during a LIVE game must be refused (conceding is how you leave one of those). The DO gates on seated + bound socket + `in-game` + a readable record whose `state.phase.kind === "gameOver"`, retiring a stale record on the way past. `commit` then sees the lobby leave in-game, `abandonsMatch` fires and `MATCH_KEY` is dropped — which is exactly what makes the next handoff mint a FRESH game instead of hitting `handoffToMatch`'s idempotency guard on the game that just ended. **Client:** the button lives INSIDE the game-over result panel (which is `pointer-events-none` so the finished board stays inspectable; the button opts back in), styled as the one primary action on the board rather than more HUD glass, because this is the one moment it applies. 1853 → **1858** tests, `bun run check` green. **BROWSER-VERIFIED on two real clients, including the griefing guard:** mid-game neither board offered it; after a concede both did; pressing it on one returned BOTH to the versus screen with decks kept, the asker readied and the opponent showing "Ready" on the other side; the opponent readying started a genuinely NEW game — the log restarted at T0 with fresh shuffles and a new coin flip, proving the record was dropped rather than re-served. And a CRAFTED mid-game `rematch`, sent over a second socket properly bound to a player's own id and account, was silently dropped with the live board untouched. Zero page errors. *Why: the cheapest correct feature is the one that adds no new state — "ready" already means "I want to play this game", so a rematch is just that flow re-entered, and the only genuinely new thing is the server-side judgement of whether the match is over, which is exactly why it is a frame and not an intent.* (2026-07-26) |
| D78 | The spectator view is `redactGame(..., spectating)` — one gate, both sides symmetric (P4 online, increment 3c-vii-a) | Spectate is the last item on the P4 plan, and it splits cleanly: this is the HIDDEN-INFORMATION half, landed on its own so it can be reviewed as a security change rather than buried in transport and UI (the 2b-iii-a precedent — the seam before its consumer). **The threat model is the decision.** A spectator is not a stranger: the realistic attacker is a PLAYER who opens a spectator view of the match they are playing to read the other hand. So the property is "a spectator sees strictly LESS than either seated player", and the two sides are treated symmetrically — `redactGame(state, side, true)` withholds BOTH hands, BOTH sides' setup placements (a seated viewer sees their own because they placed them; a spectator placed nothing, so watching from p1's side must not reveal what p1 benched) and every actor affordance. `viewerSeat` degrades from "who I am" to "which side renders at the bottom". **The mechanism matters as much as the rule:** the five `turn:action` affordance helpers already guarded on `viewerSeat !== turnSeat`, so widening that parameter to `Seat \| null` and passing ONE `actor = spectating ? null : phase.seat` withholds all five through guards that already existed — rather than five `spectating ? [] : …` branches that the next affordance added there could silently miss. That is not tidiness: `trainers`/`abilities`/`rareCandy` name cards **in the actor's hand**, so leaking them would undo the hand redaction two lines above. The `effect:choose` prompt is withheld the same way (its candidates can be the answerer's own deck cards, and a spectator can never answer). What still crosses is exactly what is public at a real table: both boards after setup, the Stadium, discards, prize/deck COUNTS, hand SIZES, whose turn it is and the outcome. Alternatives rejected: a separate `redactForSpectator` that post-processes a seated snapshot (fragile — a new leaky field passes straight through unless someone remembers to strip it), and letting a spectator see both hands behind a "trusted spectator" flag (there is no such thing when either player can open the link). engine RULES UNCHANGED (0.41.0 — a read surface, not a rules surface; `MATCH_RECORD_VERSION` untouched since neither `GameState` nor the record moved). 1858 → **1865** tests, including the one that matters: no uid private to EITHER seat appears anywhere in a spectator snapshot, asserted from both sides of the table. **NOT WIRED YET** — nothing calls it until 3c-vii-b, which is deliberately scoped to reuse the lobby link as the spectate link: a third socket whose `hello` is `reject-full` becomes a spectator IF the lobby is in-game (you can watch a match, you cannot watch a lobby), which needs no new frame and no new URL. *Why: the safe default has to be structural. Anyone can add a field to `RedactedPhase` next month; the only way that field is withheld from a spectator by default is if the withholding lives at the one place the actor is decided, not at each field.* (2026-07-26) |
| D79 | The lobby link IS the spectate link — a refused seat becomes a spectator (P4 online, increment 3c-vii-b) | Wiring 3c-vii-a's seatless redaction to real viewers, with the design constraint that it add **no new frame, no new URL and no invite flow**. The entry point is a refusal that already existed: `helloDecision` returns `reject-full` to a third player, and that branch now — **only while the lobby is `in-game`** — also marks the socket a spectator (`attachSpectator`, attachment.ts) and hands it the snapshot plus `redactGame(state, "p1", true)`. The rule falls out of the code rather than being imposed on it: **you can watch a MATCH, you cannot watch a lobby.** Outside in-game the refusal stays the dead end it has always been, which is right — a pre-match lobby has nothing to show, and a seat may yet free up for that person. **A spectator is deliberately not a participant in any sense the lobby can see:** no `playerId`, no `PlayerState`, nothing in the snapshot, so no rule anywhere depends on how many are watching, and the reducer is untouched. Their safety is structural twice over — every handler that can change the game gates on `attachedPlayer`, which stays null for them, and the board they receive is the one that withholds both hands. `attachPlayer` clears the flag, so a watcher who later takes a freed seat stops getting the seatless board. **Players are NOT told they are being watched** — chosen deliberately: telling them needs a spectator count on the wire and a broadcast on every join/leave, and the lobby already treats a refused socket silently. Recorded here so the next session can reverse it knowingly rather than discover it. **Deliberate consequence:** anyone with (or guessing) a 4-character lobby code can watch a game in progress. What they get is a public table — both boards, no hands, no deck order, no prizes — which is the intended product behaviour, not an oversight. **Client:** `useLobby`'s sticky `rejected` boolean became `refusal: "full" \| "kicked" \| null`, because the two are no longer the same thing — a KICK is terminal, while "full" keeps the connection, keeps accepting `state` (for the players' names) and `match` (the board), and flips the status to `spectating` on the first board. Its hello retry moved to the idle-driven cadence heartbeats already keep fresh, so a spectator whose socket drops re-announces and is re-marked, without becoming a retry storm against a lobby that is simply full. `OnlineMatch` gained `spectating`, which removes every control (`OnlineHud`, Concede, Rematch, drags, Pass, the forfeit banner), keeps the board, the LOG and the result, and asks a leave question that costs nothing. 1865 → **1874** tests. **BROWSER-VERIFIED with a third, signed-out profile on the same lobby link:** the board rendered; at `setup:place` the player saw their own hand as "Skeledirge; Skeledirge; Rare Candy; …" while the spectator saw "Face-down card" ×7 for BOTH hands — the hidden-information property, live; the spectator had no Concede/Ready/Rematch and a disabled Pass; a concede propagated to them instantly with the result but no Rematch; a rematch dropped them back to "Lobby is full" (the watch-a-match-not-a-lobby rule); and they picked the NEXT match up automatically when it started. Zero page errors on all three. **The run caught a real bug:** `state` frames only go out on a lobby COMMIT, so a spectator joining mid-match never received one and their board read "Host vs Guest" — `refuseSeat` now sends the snapshot explicitly. *Why: the cheapest correct entry point is one that already exists. "Refused a seat" and "may watch" are the same fact about a socket, so deriving one from the other costs a branch instead of a protocol, and the rule it implies — matches are watchable, lobbies are not — is the one you would have chosen anyway.* (2026-07-26) *Superseded in part by "Online play in the Lua frontend" (2026-10-22): the Lua client offers no spectating; a full lobby shows "Lobby is full".* |
| D80 | Half-open sockets are swept with the runtime's OWN keepalive timestamp (P4 online, increment 3c-viii) | The gap 3c-v's browser run surfaced, and the last known way an online match could stall. `setWebSocketAutoResponse` answers the client's 1.5s ping without waking the DO — the thing that makes hibernation worth having — but it also means the DO can never notice pings that STOP. A network that dies without a TCP close therefore left a socket that looked perfectly live forever: `hasSocket` said yes, no disconnect grace was armed, no forfeit clock started, and the opponent waited indefinitely. Every ORDINARY exit (tab close, browser kill, navigation, reload, crash) delivers a close event, which is why this never bit in practice and why it took a deliberate `set offline` to reproduce. **The fix is to read the signal the runtime was already recording:** `state.getWebSocketAutoResponseTimestamp(ws)` returns when it last auto-answered that socket, so liveness costs nothing at ping time. The alternative — dropping the auto-response and stamping `lastSeen` in `webSocketMessage` — was rejected on exactly that ground: it would wake the DO ~40 times a minute per socket and give up hibernation to learn what the runtime already knows. **What it costs instead is a POLL**, because a quiet in-game lobby schedules no alarm at all: `scheduleMaintenance` now pushes a `LIVENESS_SWEEP_MS` (30s) deadline whenever any socket is connected, so an occupied lobby wakes twice a minute. `SOCKET_SILENCE_MS` is 15s — ten missed pings, so a phone changing networks or a throttled background tab is not mistaken for a dead one — and a test pins the budget that matters: sweep + silence + the 5s grace all fit inside the 90s forfeit, so a dead network can never resolve slower than a closed tab. **A `null` timestamp counts as ALIVE**, deliberately: it is the state of every connection before its first ping, and nothing can distinguish "just opened" from "opened long ago and never pinged" — the cost of that reading is a socket we never sweep, versus closing a healthy one-second-old connection. **The sweep only CLOSES**; everything after it — the grace, the seat going disconnected, the forfeit — is the machinery a real close already drives, reached through the same `socketGone` (called explicitly, since a server-side `close()` delivers no `webSocketClose` event to us). 1874 → **1878** tests. **BROWSER-VERIFIED with the very reproducer that exposed the bug:** with a match live, one client was put offline with the socket left open; before this change that produced nothing at all, and now the opponent saw "disconnected — forfeits in 1:30" after ~50s (15s silence + up to 30s sweep + the 5s grace) and the countdown ran. Bringing the client back online restored it within ~10s — the channel reconnected, re-helloed, and the forfeit was cancelled — while the client that stayed connected throughout was never swept. Zero page errors. *Why: the cheapest signal is the one someone else is already recording. Hibernation exists to avoid waking on keepalives, so any fix that inspects keepalives directly is self-defeating; asking the runtime when it last answered turns a per-ping cost into a per-half-minute one.* (2026-07-26) *Superseded in part by "Database hot fixes: the shared quota, races and silent failures" (2026-10-27): a socket that never answered a keepalive no longer counts as alive.* |
| D81 | A lobby seat's name is the ACCOUNT's, resolved server-side (P5 polish, increment P5-1) | The first P5 item, and the one a player notices before anything else: after twelve P4 increments the lobby still said "Player 6046". A seat's name was whatever the client announced in its `hello` — a handle generated into localStorage, editable by anyone, belonging to nobody. It is now the account's `displayName`, read at the hello by `loadDisplayName` from the same `userId` the /ws session cookie already resolved (the second use of the lobby's one authenticated fact, after the owner-scoped deck read). **Resolving rather than trusting is the decision**, and it is not only cosmetic: the moment names stop being random, a client-supplied name is an impersonation vector — announce yourself as your opponent's handle and the versus screen agrees. The read sits deliberately AFTER the refusal branches, so a socket that gets no seat costs no D1 query however often it retries, and hellos are rare in steady state (heartbeats keep the client's idle timer fresh, so it only re-announces after a real gap). **The fallback rule is the careful part:** a blank account name yields `null`, and the caller drops back to the client's announced handle — never to the EMAIL, which is the one substitution that suggests itself because `AccountButton` uses exactly that. That control shows a user their OWN address; a lobby name is broadcast to the opponent and, since 3c-vii, to spectators. A cosmetic blank is a smaller problem than a published email address, and a test pins it. The client announces its account name as well, which changes nothing authoritative — it only spares the host the half-second where their own seat reads "Player 4271" before the first broadcast corrects it, since the waiting screen is seeded locally. No server-side length cap: registration bounds names at 64, `PlayerPanel` already truncates, and mangling someone's name to fit a panel is the UI's job, not the wire's. 1878 → **1881** tests, `bun run check` green. **BROWSER-VERIFIED on four clients:** two signed-in seats read "Ash" and "Misty" across the player panels, the turn banner, the game log ("Ash wins the toss") and the spectator pill ("Watching — Ash vs Misty"), while two SIGNED-OUT players kept their generated handles with the lobby working normally. Zero page errors. *Why: a name is identity, and identity that the client asserts is not identity at all. The server already knew who the socket was — using that costs one query and closes an impersonation hole that only existed because nobody's name meant anything yet.* (2026-07-26) |
| D82 | Generated avatars from a hashed account id, not uploads (P5 polish, increment P5-2) | The other half of "lobby identity", and split from the uploads polish.md describes on purpose: an upload flow means R2, a CSP change, moderation questions and a settings surface, while what the lobby actually lacked was any face at all — `PlayerPanel` drew the same grey figure for everyone. **A generated avatar buys most of the value for none of that infrastructure:** `PlayerState.avatarSeed` carries a token the server derives at the hello from the account behind the socket, and `PlayerAvatar` draws a deterministic two-hue gradient with the account's initial. Nothing is stored, nothing is uploaded, and the property that matters holds — the same person looks the same to you in every lobby, which is what makes it identity rather than decoration (browser-verified across two clients: both saw identical gradients for the same accounts, in opposite seats). **It is a HASH of the account id, never the id.** Not because a uuid is guessable, but because the lobby snapshot is BROADCAST — to the opponent, and since 3c-vii to spectators — and an internal primary key is the wrong thing to publish there: it invites joining this seat to any other surface exposing the same id. FNV-1a is enough because this is a display token, not a security boundary; the client's only use is picking colours. **An anonymous seat gets NO seed and keeps the generic figure**, deliberately: the only id available for one is the per-lobby playerId, and a face derived from that would change on the player's next visit — promising an identity that isn't there is worse than admitting there is none. **Saturation and lightness are fixed**, so the seed picks the hue and never how loud the result is; that is what stops one player's face glowing next to another's in a UI built on restrained glass. An empty name draws a bare gradient rather than a placeholder letter, since a wrong initial is a claim and a gradient is not. The wire field cost one line in `readSnapshot`'s `withSeatDefaults` — the normalizer 3c-v added for exactly this, so a lobby persisted by the previous deploy survives the new field instead of being evicted. 1881 → **1889** tests. **BROWSER-VERIFIED on four clients:** two signed-in seats drew stable, visually distinct faces ("A" orange→amber, "M" cyan→indigo) with byte-identical gradients on both clients, and two signed-out seats kept the generic figure. A screenshot confirmed they sit in-palette against both the blue and red seat washes. *Why: the expensive half of avatars is storage and moderation; the valuable half is that people become recognisable. Deriving a face from the account you already authenticated delivers the second without buying the first, and leaves the upload path a strict improvement rather than a rewrite.* (2026-07-26) |
| D83 | A deck's cover is its highest-HP Pokémon, derived on read (P5 polish, increment P5-3) | The decks grid drew the same blue card back for every deck under a generated tint, while the catalog has had real scans since P2. **The slice's whole content is the RULE**, so it lives in a pure `pickCoverCardId` that can be argued with and tested rather than inside SQL: **the Pokémon with the highest printed HP**, ties broken by copies, then by card id for determinism. HP is the best proxy the catalog offers for "the card this deck is built around", and crucially it is one number rather than a taxonomy to interpret — an ex/V/VMAX or a Stage 2 outranks the Basics that fetch it WITHOUT anyone having to rank `stage` values against each other. The two obvious alternatives both fail on a real deck: ranking by STAGE gets a Basic-ex deck wrong (a 330 HP Basic ex is the payoff and would lose to any Stage 2 tech line), and ranking by COUNT gets nearly every evolution deck wrong (the 4-of Basic beats the 3-of Stage 2 it evolves into — verified live: Skeledirge ×3 correctly beat Fuecoco ×4). Cards with no scan are skipped rather than picked and rendered blank, and a deck with no qualifying card gets `coverCardId: null`, keeping the card back — which is also exactly right for a deck you have only just created (`fromDeck`, the create-response mapper, hardcodes null for that reason). **Derived on READ**, in one extra query for the whole library beside the existing `cardCount` aggregate: no stored column, so a cover follows the deck's contents with nothing to keep in sync, and no write path to change. It is a HEURISTIC and will occasionally pick a wall over the attacker beside it; the proper answer is letting the owner choose, which this leaves room for (a stored choice would simply win over it). **A browser run caught what tests could not:** the deck NAME, white text that had always sat on a flat blue back, was nearly illegible over flames and holo foil — so the label gained a gradient scrim, applied ONLY when there is art, so a coverless deck looks exactly as it did. 1889 → **1899** tests. *Why: the cheapest correct default beats a configuration surface nobody has asked for yet, and HP is the rare heuristic that is both trivially cheap and right on the decks that matter.* (2026-07-26) |
| D84 | The playmat's pass + history controls are DELETED, not relocated (P5 polish, increment P5-4) | The oldest open defect in the repo — "the Pass turn button is unclickable while the turn panel is open", carried since 2026-07-26 with a residual about the history arrows — closed by reading the code rather than the symptom. **Two findings changed the slice entirely.** First, `PreviousButton` and `ForwardButton` **took no props at all**: no `onClick`, no handler, on any surface, ever. They were chrome from the /simulator visual prototype, so the residual ("history navigation stays unreachable during your own turn:action") described navigation that DOES NOT EXIST — and three sessions of docs, mine included, had been treating them as blocked functionality. Two buttons a screen reader announces as "Previous"/"Forward" that do nothing are worse than no buttons: they promise a feature. They are gone, to return WIRED if a real history is ever built. Second, the ⟶ pass control is enabled **exactly when the turn panel renders over it** — the panel appears precisely when `passDisabled === false` — so on /play and online it was never clickable, and relocating it would have been moving a control whose replacement (each panel's own `Pass`) already exists and is browser-verified. **The API expresses the rule instead of a flag:** `PlaymatView.onPassTurn` became OPTIONAL, and the control renders only for a driver that passes one — "give the board a pass control by giving it something to call". The /simulator sandbox passes one (nothing covers it there); /play and online do not, and shed their now-dead `passTurn`/`passDisabled` wiring with it. Rejected: moving the controls to the left edge (the game log already owns bottom-left, and moving DEAD arrows would make them more prominent), and capping the panel's height (a real cost — a long attack/ability list gains a scrollbar — paid to keep a button nobody can use). **Browser-verified at 1280×700 with a real `elementFromPoint` hit-test**, because a synthetic `.click()` is precisely what disguised this bug for weeks: at a live turn:action, /play has no "Pass turn" and no arrows, its in-panel `Pass` hit-tests to itself and advances the turn (TURN 01 → 02); /simulator still has its ⟶, hit-testing to itself. The `verify` skill's entry — which had already been corrected once, from "an agent-browser quirk" to "a real product bug" — is now corrected again to describe the current controls and, more usefully, to state the general rule: eval may DRIVE, but only a hit-test may conclude a control works. 1899 tests, unchanged in count (one test switched from clicking the unreachable ⟶ to the panel's `Pass`). *Why: the fix for a control that cannot be reached is not a better position for it. Once one of the two was never wired and the other was permanently redundant, the honest change was subtraction — and the API that falls out ("pass a handler, get a button") is simpler than the one that was there.* (2026-07-26) |
| D85 | Your account is editable on /settings, and the avatar seed rides on `User` (P5 polish, increment P5-5) | P5-1 made a lobby seat's name the ACCOUNT's `displayName`, resolved server-side and broadcast to the opponent and to spectators — and left a player wearing whatever register picked, with nowhere to change it. **`PATCH /auth/me` + a Profile section on /settings** closes that: one required field (`updateProfileRequestSchema`, trim + 1–64, the same bounds register uses on its OPTIONAL name), `requireUser` BEFORE the validator so a signed-out caller hears "unauthorized" rather than a critique of their body, and no write at all when the name is unchanged. **The interesting decision is the FACE.** A profile that showed a name but no avatar would be a text box calling itself a profile, and one drawing a lookalike avatar would be worse — it would promise a face that isn't the one opponents see. So `userSchema` gained **`avatarSeed`**, derived by the same `avatarSeedFrom` D82 introduced, and the section renders the SAME `PlayerAvatar` the lobby does. That moved two things deliberately: the module to `apps/api/src/auth/avatar.ts` (the seed is a property of the ACCOUNT, not of a seat — lobby already imports from auth, not the reverse) and the component to `src/components/` (shared, so /settings and the lobby cannot drift into two similar drawings). A test pins `avatarSeedOf` against `avatarSeedFrom` for the same reason. **Deriving the seed client-side was rejected**: a second copy of FNV-1a in the web bundle is a hash that must agree with the server forever, and the day it doesn't a player's own face silently stops matching the one everyone else sees. Publishing it costs nothing — the lobby already broadcasts it. Smaller calls: the avatar's initial follows the DRAFT as you type, so the face and the field can never disagree on screen; a 401 on save re-probes the session so the panel becomes the sign-in prompt, which says it better than an error line under a dead form; the form is keyed by `user.id` so switching accounts re-seeds the field. A rename reaches a lobby at the next hello (`loadDisplayName` resolves the seat name there), so a player already seated elsewhere keeps the old name until they rejoin. 1899 → **1911** tests. **BROWSER-VERIFIED on two clients:** renaming "Brock" → "Pewter Brock" flipped the initial to "P" live, kept the gradient byte-identical (the seed is the account id, not the name), updated the corner account control with no reload, survived a reload, and — the point of the slice — **the opponent's client showed "Pewter Brock" with that same gradient**. Save and the field both hit-tested to themselves; the signed-out panel offers a sign-in link whose `state.from` returns to /settings. Zero page errors. *Why: an identity you cannot change isn't yours. And once the profile's job is "how you appear to other players", showing anything other than the exact artifacts the lobby broadcasts would make the page a mock-up of itself.* (2026-07-26) |
| D86 | A deck cover is a PIN over the derived default, resolved on read (P5 polish, increment P5-6) | P5-3 fronted every deck with its highest-HP Pokémon and said outright that the proper answer was letting the owner choose. This is that: a nullable `decks.cover_card_id` the editor sets, and a pure **`effectiveCoverCardId(chosen, candidates)`** the list query runs — **the pin wins, and everything else falls through to `pickCoverCardId`**. The derived rule is untouched; it is now explicitly the DEFAULT rather than the answer. **Three decisions carry the slice.** First, **the resolution runs against the candidate list the default was already deriving from**, so honouring a choice costs NO extra query, and a pin that has gone stale (its card removed elsewhere, or its scan gone after a re-ingest) silently falls back instead of drawing a blank rectangle — a deck must never front a card it doesn't run. Second, **the pin may be a TRAINER or an Energy while the derived default stays Pokémon-only**: guessing needs a rule, choosing does not, and a signature Supporter is a legitimate face (browser-verified with Academy at Night, which the rule could never pick). Third, **two fields, deliberately not one**: `DeckSummary.coverCardId` remains the face being DRAWN (so the grid needed no change at all), while the full `Deck` carries `chosenCoverCardId`, the stored CHOICE the editor's picker is set to — null for almost every deck. Collapsing them into one name would have made "what is drawn" and "what was chosen" the same word for two different questions. **`Automatic` is an explicit option in the picker**, not the absence of one, so reverting is one click; it deliberately shows no art, because the default is a RULE and drawing today's answer to it would read as "this card". **The consistency rule lives on both sides:** PATCH validates the pin against the card list the patch LEAVES BEHIND (400 `cover card not in deck`) and drops a pin whose card that patch removes, while the editor's `toSnapshot` drops it locally too — not cosmetic, since the cover rides the same debounced PATCH as the card list, and a self-contradicting body would 400 forever (the saver retries the same dirty snapshot). The cover joining that one save path is what keeps the pair atomic. 1911 → **1932** tests; migration `0004`. **BROWSER-VERIFIED end to end at 1920×1080:** pinning the Trainer put it on the library tile, removing it dropped the pin, kept saving ("Saved", PATCH 200 — the wedge that would have followed a naive implementation) and fell back to Absol; a pin survived a reload through GET /decks/:id; a hand-crafted PATCH naming a card outside the deck got the 400; and both the footer control and a picker tile hit-tested to themselves. *Why: a heuristic that is right most of the time plus an override that is always right beats trying to make the heuristic cleverer — and the override is only trustworthy if the two can never disagree about what the deck contains.* (2026-07-26) |
| D87 | A favourite deck is a validated PIN of the account's OWN deck, resolved on read (P5 polish, increment P5-7) | Joins P5-5 (an editable profile) and P5-6 (a deck's cover art): an account pins one of its own decks as its favourite and sees that deck's cover + name on /settings. A nullable **`users.favourite_deck_id`** (migration `0005`) surfaced as **`userSchema.favouriteDeckId`** — **just the ID.** **Three choices.** First, **store only the id, not the deck's name/cover:** /settings resolves those from the caller's own `GET /decks` list, so a rename or re-cover shows through with no stale copy and a favourite whose deck has vanished resolves to a blank tile — the SAME "derive on read, tolerate stale" the covers use (D83/D86). Second, **a favourite must be one of YOUR decks:** `PATCH /auth/me` gains an OPTIONAL nullable `favouriteDeckId` (absent leaves it, null clears it, a string is validated against the caller's decks) and 400s `favourite deck not found` for anyone else's id or a phantom — the account can never point at a deck it doesn't own, mirroring D86's `cover card not in deck`. Third, **the FK is `ON DELETE SET NULL`** so deleting a favourited deck clears the pin rather than blocking the delete (a RESTRICT FK would) or dangling a stale id; D1 enforces FKs here (the deck DELETE leans on `deck_cards`' cascade), and since drizzle-kit drops the referential action on an ADD COLUMN, migration 0005 restores `ON DELETE SET NULL` by hand. The users↔decks mutual reference forced an `AnySQLiteColumn` annotation to break the TS type cycle. The picker is a native `<select>` in the SAME form as the name, riding ONE PATCH so name + favourite save atomically; the provider adopts the answer, so the corner control follows with no re-probe. 1932 → **1936** tests. Browser-verified end to end 2026-07-27 (build #71): pin on /settings → cover (`sv06.5-012`, real art) + name render + `PATCH /auth/me` 200; survives reload; `DELETE`ing the deck clears `favouriteDeckId` to null server-side (the FK) and the tile goes blank. Also covered by a DOM round-trip test + schema units. *Why: the profile only needs to REFERENCE a deck, and the deck already owns its name and cover — copying them onto the account would just be a second source of truth to keep in sync; the id plus resolve-on-read is the whole feature, and the validated pin + SET NULL make "your favourite is always a real deck of yours" true by construction.* (2026-07-27) |
| D88 | A card detail view is a read-only modal hydrated on demand from the full-card endpoint (P5 polish, increment P5-8) | Cards in the deck builder had only ever been an add-a-copy tile; this adds a **detail modal** opened from the grid that shows the full card (attacks, abilities, weakness/resistance/retreat, illustrator, rarity, regulation mark, Trainer/Energy rules text). The backend was already complete — a `GET /cards/:id` returning the full `cardSchema`, and a battle-tested `getCard` client — so the whole slice is web-UI; nothing in API/DB/schema/ingest changed. **Four decisions.** First, **the inspect affordance is a SIBLING of the add button, never a child:** the grid tile is a `<button>` (click adds, right-click removes, pointer-down drags) and a button-in-button is invalid HTML, so a corner info button floats over the tile at `z-20`, revealed on tile hover and its own keyboard focus; clicking it opens the modal and does NOT add a copy, and the three existing tile gestures are untouched. Second, **seed + fetch, and the fetch must never blank the seed:** the header name and big image come from the `BuilderCard` the grid already holds (up the instant the dialog opens) while the body swaps loading→ready as `getCard` resolves; a `cancelled` flag drops a stale response so reopening a different card can't be clobbered by a slower earlier fetch, and the seed is retained through the close so nothing flashes empty. Third, **honest about what the catalog stores:** pricing, national-dex number and flavor text are NOT in D1 (see the `cards` schema header), so the view doesn't show them, and the set prints as raw `{localId} · {setId}` rather than a name lookup the payload can't back; every block is guarded so a Trainer/Energy omits what it lacks. Fourth, **the new surface respects `prefers-reduced-motion`** (`motion-reduce:transition-none`, no scale under reduced motion) — a down-payment on the reduced-motion-audit item, and the model the older P5 surfaces (CoverDialog, PlayerAvatar) still need. Type/cost pips reuse `EnergyGlyph` behind a pure exported `isGlyphType` guard that text-chips anything outside the ten glyph keys. 1936 → **1941** tests (a jsdom dialog test + the guard). Built via sub-agents; main session reviewed the diff — self-reviewed, no independent reviewer. **BROWSER-VERIFIED end to end 2026-07-27 (build #72):** hover revealed the corner button; clicking "View Absol details" opened the modal (name instant) then `GET /cards/:id` (200) filled Attacks/Combat/Illus + a high-quality `<img>` (`naturalWidth=600`) from the R2 mirror; the info click left "Add Absol" at 0-in-deck; a Trainer refetched to show only its Effect; X/Escape/backdrop all closed it; zero console errors. *Why: the data already existed one endpoint away — the only real question was interaction, and making inspect a peer of add (not a mode on it) keeps the fast add-a-copy loop intact while giving the card a place to be read; hydrate-on-open with a stale-guard is the same "derive on read" discipline the rest of P5 uses, and refusing to invent pricing/set-name keeps the view trustworthy.* (2026-07-27) *Superseded in part by D90: the reduced-motion guard moved from the call site into the `glass.ts` constants.* |
| D89 | Carry `prefers-reduced-motion` through the remaining new-P5 surfaces by appending `motion-reduce:*` variants AT THE CALL SITE (P5 polish, increment P5-9) | Three new P5 surfaces still animated unconditionally, and the app's ONLY global reduced-motion rule (`src/index.css` L190) neutralises just the `<body>` glow transition and the `.route-view` route-fade — it never touches a Tailwind `transition-*`/`scale-*`/`animate-*` utility on a component, so those surfaces were unguarded. An Explore audit found the offenders on `CoverDialog.tsx` (`TILE_BASE`'s `hover:scale-[1.03] active:scale-95`, the close button's `transition-colors`, the Done ghost button), `Settings.tsx` (the font `<select>`, the back-arrow icon's `transition-transform` slide, the Back-to-home ghost button) and `ProfileSection.tsx` (the Save button, the sign-in link); **`PlayerAvatar` was already clean** (its only `style` is a static gradient). Each got `motion-reduce:transition-none` appended, matching the **D88 idiom exactly**: the P5-8 author deliberately patched at the CALL SITE rather than editing the shared `glass.ts` constants, and this slice honours that placement (the alternative — guarding the constants — is written up as the next resume point, see below). **One judgement call:** the Save spinner is a perpetual `animate-spin`, which `transition-none` cannot stop, so it got **`motion-reduce:animate-none`** — the one genuinely-new variant this codebase hadn't used. Halting the spin is the correct accessible behaviour: the "Save" text plus the disabled/dimmed button already convey the in-progress state, and `SpinnerIcon` is a 3/4-ring arc that reads fine frozen. Pure CSS-class slice — no logic, component, or API/DB/schema touch; 1941 → 1941 tests (nothing new to unit-test: `motion-reduce:*` variants aren't observable in jsdom and asserting className strings is brittle). `bun run check` green (137 files / 1941 tests); engine rules unchanged (0.41.0). **Verified via the shipped stylesheet, not a browser drive:** a production `vite build` emits BOTH `motion-reduce:transition-none` and `motion-reduce:animate-none` inside `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)`, proving the guards are live and not a silently-dropped typo'd variant — the one real failure mode for a class-only change; a full browser drive was judged low-value since the change is purely additive + reduced-motion-only (it cannot regress the default render) and the `transition-none` mechanism is already browser-verified from P5-8. **Deliberately deferred (the D89 resume point):** the ROOT CAUSE — the shared `glass.ts` button constants (`GLASS_GHOST_BUTTON`, `GLASS_ACCENT_BUTTON`, `GLASS_HUD_BUTTON`, `GLASS_NEUTRAL_BUTTON`, `GLASS_DIALOG_GHOST_BUTTON`, `GLASS_FILTER_TOGGLE`, `GLASS_INPUT`) all carry `transition-*` + `active:scale-95` with no reduced-motion guard and feed EVERY glass button app-wide, so the same defect still lives outside the P5 surfaces; adding `motion-reduce:transition-none` INTO those constants would fix the whole app in one additive edit, but it reverses this decision's call-site placement and reaches well beyond the P5 scope, so it's a deliberate app-wide pass worth the user's nod. *Why: the resume point scoped this to the three P5 surfaces and D88 set the call-site precedent; honouring that keeps the slice small, low-risk and non-reversing, while the root-cause `glass.ts` fix is flagged loudly as the natural, bigger follow-up rather than silently smuggled into a "P5 surfaces" slice.* **REVERSED by D90 (P5-10) — the constant-level guard was done and the call-site placement is no longer the precedent for these constants.** (2026-07-27) |
| D90 | Guard `prefers-reduced-motion` at the SOURCE — put `motion-reduce:transition-none` INTO the shared `glass.ts` constants, reversing D88/D89's call-site placement (P5 polish, increment P5-10) | The root cause D89 flagged and wrote up as its resume point, now fixed. The seven `glass.ts` constants that carry a transition — `GLASS_HUD_BUTTON`, `GLASS_GHOST_BUTTON`, `GLASS_DIALOG_GHOST_BUTTON`, `GLASS_ACCENT_BUTTON`, `GLASS_NEUTRAL_BUTTON`, `GLASS_FILTER_TOGGLE` (all `transition-all` + `active:scale-95`) and `GLASS_INPUT` (`transition-colors`) — each got `motion-reduce:transition-none` appended, so EVERY glass control app-wide (auth, lobby HUD, filters, decks, dialogs, corner controls) respects reduced motion from the constant rather than each call site; one additive edit covers the whole app. Under reduced motion the fade is off and the `active:scale-95` SNAPS rather than animating — the same behaviour `CoverDialog`'s `TILE_BASE` was browser-verified with in P5-8, so this is not a new interaction, only a wider one. **This REVERSES D88/D89's "patch at the call site" placement** for these constants; the reversal is deliberate and was named as the blessed next step in D89's own resume point, so it is a trailed reversal, not a silent one. A header comment in `glass.ts` now states the invariant ("every constant with a `transition-*` also carries `motion-reduce:transition-none`") so the next author keeps new transitional constants guarded. **Because the constant now supplies the guard, the four call-site guards that sat adjacent to a glass constant became redundant and were dropped:** `CardDetailDialog` Retry (`GLASS_GHOST_BUTTON`), `Settings` Back-to-home (`GLASS_GHOST_BUTTON`), `CoverDialog` Done (`GLASS_DIALOG_GHOST_BUTTON`), `ProfileSection` Save (`GLASS_ACCENT_BUTTON`). **Every call-site guard protecting an OWN non-constant transition was KEPT** — `Settings` font `<select>` + back-arrow icon slide, `CoverDialog` `TILE_BASE` + close button, `CardDetailDialog` close button, `CardGrid` info button, `ProfileSection` sign-in link, and the Save spinner's `motion-reduce:animate-none` (a `transition-none` cannot stop an `animate-spin`). Pure CSS-class slice — no logic/component/API/DB/schema touch; 1941 → 1941 tests (nothing new to unit-test: `motion-reduce:*` isn't observable in jsdom and className assertions are brittle). `bun run check` green (137 files / 1941 tests); engine rules unchanged (0.41.0). **Verified via the shipped stylesheet, not a browser drive:** a production `vite build` emits `.motion-reduce\:transition-none{transition-property:none}` (beside `.motion-reduce\:animate-none`) inside `@media (prefers-reduced-motion:reduce)` in the main `index-*.css` bundle — the shared chunk where the glass buttons live — proving the guard is live, not a silently-dropped typo'd variant. A browser drive was judged low-value: the change is purely additive + reduced-motion-only (cannot regress the default render), the mechanism is browser-verified from P5-8, and the constant-vs-call-site placement is invisible in the compiled CSS (cascade is by stylesheet order, not class-attribute order, so moving the class from the call site into the constant string cannot change the result). *Why: D89 deliberately chose the small call-site slice and flagged the root-cause constant fix as the natural follow-up "worth the user's nod (or a considered autonomous slice)"; the autoloop resume point picked it up as that considered slice. Fixing the source and dropping the now-redundant per-call-site guards leaves ONE place the reduced-motion guard lives for glass controls, which is more maintainable than N call sites drifting out of sync — the exact reason these constants exist. With P5-10, P5's autonomous surface is exhausted: the only open P5 items (uploaded avatars, the remote P4 deploy) both need the user.* (2026-07-27) |
| D91 | Defiance Band — a CONDITIONAL passive damage bonus, the D40 `BoardCondition` vocabulary's third consumer (P3-M5, engine 0.42.0) | The `conditionalDamageBonus` backlog row's first card, and the first PASSIVE reader of the D40 vocabulary — the conditional twin of Vitality Band ("If you have more Prize cards remaining than your opponent, the attacks of the Pokémon this card is attached to do 30 more damage to your opponent's Active Pokémon (before applying Weakness and Resistance)", sv01-169). **A card row + one new field, not a new primitive**, because it REUSES the existing `morePrizesThanOpponent` condition (added at D40 and already read by Fighting Au Lait's play gate + a branch op — this is the same predicate as a passive). `PassiveEffects` gains **`damageBonusBeforeWRIf: { amount, cond: BoardCondition }`**. **The load-bearing design call: WHERE the condition is evaluated.** continuous.ts's `passivesOf` is the single continuous-modifier aggregation point but is SEAT-FREE (it takes only `state` + the Pokémon), and a `BoardCondition` is seat-relative (whose Prizes?). Folding the evaluation into `passivesOf` would have needed `conditionHolds` from interpreter.ts, and interpreter.ts already imports `passivesOf` — a MODULE CYCLE. So `passivesOf` stays pure and seat-free, now collecting the gated bonuses RAW in a new `conditionalDamageBonusBeforeWR: {amount,cond}[]` (aggregation is its job); the FOLD lives in a new **`interpreter.ts` `attackerPreWRBonus(state, pokemon, seat)`** (evaluation beside the sole `conditionHolds`, its job), shared by the ONLY two pre-W/R read sites — attack.ts's main hit (`action.seat`) and `snipeActive` (`ctx.seat`) — the only places that know the attacker's seat. Kept OUT of the unconditional `damageBonusBeforeWR` so the defender-reduction and HP reads (which never look at either) can never be credited an attacker's conditional bonus. **snipeActive parity is free**: it now calls the shared helper, so a snipe landing on the opponent's Active gets Defiance Band's +30 exactly as it already got Vitality Band's — verified by the existing Vitality Band snipe coverage still passing after the refactor. **Test-deck choice (a small but real call):** the new tests use a DEDICATED `DEFIANCE_DECK` fixture rather than adding sv01-169 to the shared `STADIUM_TOOL_DECK` — the latter is a fixed 60 (setup.ts enforces `DECK_SIZE`), so any card swapped in reshuffles every seed and broke a fragile opening-hand test (fix-energy-in-hand + fix-tool-in-deck) shared across 5 suites; a dedicated all-Basic deck is mulligan-free and seed-independent (cards pulled post-setup by id). **No web/API/DB/schema touch** — damage bonuses are engine-internal, surfaced only via the `DAMAGE_DEALT` log event (no projection field), confirmed by an Explore map. engineVersion 0.41.0 → **0.42.0**; 1941 → **1945** tests (registration + applies-when-behind + no-op-when-level + the (30+30)×2=120 before-W/R pin). `bun run check` green (137 files / 1945 tests). **Self-reviewed, no independent reviewer** (a cheap, contained slice; the mechanism mirrors Vitality Band, already reviewed). **Remaining on the row: Choice Belt** (sv02-176) — a TARGET-TYPE gate ("…Active Pokémon V"), a different shape (not a `BoardCondition`, so not this field), its own slice. *Why: the resume point named this "the last cheap D40 reader"; it is cheap precisely because the hard part (the condition vocabulary) already exists — the only new engineering is deciding where a seat-relative predicate gets evaluated within a seat-free aggregation pipeline, resolved by splitting aggregation (continuous.ts, unchanged responsibility) from evaluation (interpreter.ts, beside the one evaluator) rather than threading a seat through six call sites or duplicating `conditionHolds`.* (2026-07-27) |
| D92 | Choice Belt — a TARGET-gated passive damage bonus, and the rule-box suffix read off the NAME (P3-M5, engine 0.43.0) | The `conditionalDamageBonus` row's **second and last card, closing it 2/2** — the target-gated twin of Vitality Band: "The attacks of the Pokémon this card is attached to do 30 more damage to your opponent's Active Pokémon **V** (before applying Weakness and Resistance)" (sv02-176). **Two calls carry it.** **(1) A SIBLING field, not the D40 vocabulary.** `PassiveEffects` gains **`damageBonusBeforeWRIfTarget: { amount, targetSuffix }`** beside D91's `damageBonusBeforeWRIf`, because the two gates ask different questions: D91's reads the ATTACKER's seat-relative board (a `BoardCondition`), this one reads the card being HIT. Folding it into the `BoardCondition` union would have meant inventing a condition whose subject is the defender — a different kind of predicate wearing the same name. The plumbing is D91's, unchanged in shape: the seat-free `passivesOf` collects these bonuses RAW into a new `targetConditionalDamageBonusBeforeWR` list (aggregation), and the FOLD stays in `interpreter.ts`'s `attackerPreWRBonus`, which now also takes the **`defenderCard`** — free, because both of the only two pre-W/R read sites already had it in hand (attack.ts's main hit had `defenderCard`, `snipeActive` had `target.card`), so **snipeActive parity is again free** and the module-cycle argument that put the fold in interpreter.ts still holds. **(2) The "is it a V" test needed NO schema, ingest, DB or API change.** tcgdex's dedicated `suffix` field is dropped at ingest — but the marker is ALSO part of the printed card NAME (a Pokémon V is literally printed "<Species> V"), and `name` IS persisted, so the datum survives *encoded in a column we already have*. A new **`cards.ts pokemonSuffixOf(card): "ex"\|"V"\|"VMAX"\|"VSTAR"\|"GX"\|null`** derives it, matching each suffix as a WHOLE marker: "V" does not match "VMAX"/"VSTAR" (neither is a space-then-"V"), which is exactly the distinction the card draws — Choice Belt boosts a V, never a VMAX. The alternative (an ingest change + a migration + a backfill to persist `suffix`) would have been a multi-layer slice to recover a datum already in hand. It is a derivation, not a guess: the suffix is part of the printed name by the game's own naming rule. **Known and accepted: the gate is DORMANT against the live catalog.** The ingested SV-era pool contains no Pokémon V at all, so today Choice Belt is registered, correct, and never fires in a real game; it starts firing the moment a prior-era set is ingested, with no engine change. That is the right trade — the card is in the catalog, and the alternative is a registry hole that reads as "unsimulated". **A follow-up it unlocks (recorded, NOT taken):** `prizeValueOf` still returns 1 for every KO, its comment blaming the lost suffix; `pokemonSuffixOf` now supplies exactly the classification it wants, so 2-prize ex/V and 3-prize VMAX become a ONE-LINE change. Deliberately not taken here — prize counts ripple through the win condition and almost every KO test, which is its own slice, not a rider on a Tool. **No web/API/DB/schema touch** (damage bonuses are engine-internal, surfaced only via the `DAMAGE_DEALT` log event). engineVersion 0.42.0 → **0.43.0**; 1945 → **1952** tests — 5 behavioural (registration, +30 vs a V, no-op vs a plain body, **no-op vs a VMAX**, and the (30+30)×2=120 before-W/R pin against a Fire-weak V) on a dedicated `CHOICE_DECK` fixture per D91's test-deck reasoning, plus 2 `pokemonSuffixOf` unit tests in `cards.test.ts` (its conventional home) pinning the literal match and the null cases. `bun run check` green (137 files / 1952 tests). **Self-reviewed, no independent reviewer** — a contained slice reusing D91's just-reviewed mechanism. *Why: the row's last card looked like it needed persisted card metadata we threw away at ingest; it turned out the metadata is redundantly encoded in the name, so the whole slice collapsed to one derivation function and one passive field.* (2026-07-27) |
| D93 | Prize value — a KO owes its rule box's real Prize count, off `pokemonSuffixOf` (P3-M5, engine 0.44.0) | The follow-up D92 recorded-but-did-not-take. `prizeValueOf` stops returning 1 for every KO: **3 Prizes for a VMAX, 2 for any other rule box (ex / V / VSTAR / GX), 1 for a plain body** — `suffix === null ? 1 : suffix === "VMAX" ? 3 : 2`, the class read off D92's **`pokemonSuffixOf`** (the printed NAME suffix, so — like Choice Belt — **no schema / ingest / DB / API / web change** was needed to value an ex at its two Prizes). This is the first D92-family change that actually FIRES against the live catalog, which is full of ex Pokémon (Choice Belt stays dormant for want of a Pokémon V; prize value does not). **The load-bearing call was NOT the code — it was measuring the blast radius instead of trusting the fear.** D92's own comment (and the `prizeValueOf` doc) warned the change "ripples through the win condition and almost every KO test," which is why three prior sessions deferred it. So rather than guess, this session **applied the one-line change and ran the FULL suite: all 1952 tests stayed green.** The reason is structural — every existing KO test knocks out a PLAIN body (the `fix-victim` / `fix-attacker` fixtures), and `prizeValueOf(plain) === 1` is unchanged; the ex/V/VMAX fixtures exist only as ATTACKERS or as Choice Belt's damage TARGET, never as a KO'd body a test counts Prizes for. The feared radius was real in principle and empty in fact. **No flow change needed either:** the `count` these feed was ALWAYS a variable at both prize read sites (flow.ts's `takePrizes` stage at :92/:107 and the §14 tie-guard speculation at :175-:205), and both already clamp to the Prizes that remain (the handler's `Math.min(stage.count, side.prizes.length)`, the tie-guard's `prizes.slice(count)`), so a 2-Prize KO with one Prize left takes the last one and WINS (§14.1) rather than over-drawing — `prizeValueOf` simply never fed those paths a value >1 before. **Tests:** a dedicated **`prizeValue.test.ts`** — 4 behavioural (V→2, VMAX→3, plain→1 as the contrast, and the clamp-to-win) driving P1's `fix-attacker` Bite through a pre-damaged rule-box Active on a new mulligan-free **`PRIZE_DECK`** (D91's dedicated-deck reasoning: never mutate a shared fixed-60), plus 2 `prizeValueOf` unit cases in `cards.test.ts`. The stale `attack.test.ts` ex-flow-pick comment ("the catalog cannot mark ex yet") was corrected — that test crafts a 2-Prize decision directly to pin the PICK HANDLER in isolation, which is still the right split now that `prizeValue.test.ts` owns the KO→count wiring. **No web/API/DB/schema touch** (the web HUD renders whatever Prize row the engine reports; a 2-Prize KO just removes two). engineVersion 0.43.0 → **0.44.0**; 1952 → **1958** tests (137 → **138** files). `bun run check` green. **Self-reviewed, no independent reviewer** — a one-function slice reusing D92's just-reviewed `pokemonSuffixOf`. *Why: the datum and the classifier already existed (D92); the only thing standing between them and correct Prize counts was an unmeasured fear of test churn, and measuring it dissolved the fear — a good reminder that "wide blast radius" is a hypothesis to test, not a reason to defer indefinitely.* (2026-07-27) |
| D94 | Spread "also" rider — one optional token, and a deviation from the resume point's ordering toward the candidate with real yield (P3-M5, engine 0.45.0) | The each-of-spread also-rider: "This attack **also** does N damage to each of your opponent's Benched Pokémon." (Cetoddle sv02-053 "Avalanche" — 30 to the Active, then 10 to each Bench). **A one-token regex relaxation, ZERO new op/interpreter/registry code:** the word "also" is made OPTIONAL in the existing `SPREAD_EACH_BENCH` deriver (`^This attack (?:also )?does …`), so the rider derives to the **same** `spreadDamage` op as the bare "This attack does N …" form. **Design call — one rule, not a twin.** The snipe family has separate anchored regexes per shape because "1 of" vs "each of" pick genuinely different ops (`damageChosen` vs `spreadDamage`); here the string AFTER "does" is identical, and the engine already treats even the bare spread as a SECONDARY hit (the main damage rides the attack's own `damage` field — comment predates this slice), so "also" is pure emphasis of a fact the `damage` field already carries. A separate `ALSO_SPREAD_EACH_BENCH` regex + dispatch case would be a redundant twin producing a byte-identical op, so it was rejected in favour of the optional token. **A test moved, not just added:** `alsoBenchedSnipe.test.ts` listed this exact "each of" text as a `toBeNull()` near-miss — but it was null only because NO reader accepted it; now `SPREAD_EACH_BENCH` correctly claims it, so the assertion flipped from "refused" to "derives to `spreadDamage`", preserving the test's real intent (the single-target `damageChosen` snipe reader must never grab a spread). Pinned by a derive assertion on the verbatim Cetoddle text in `knockout.test.ts`; the full §8.5 spread flow (bench W/R, Bouffer reduction, bench KO) is already covered by `fix-sniper`'s Spread Shot, so re-driving an identical op through a Cetoddle deck would be redundant. **The DEVIATION worth recording:** the resume point ranked "count-2" (a benched/any-target snipe to "2 of your opponent's Pokémon") FIRST as cheapest. But there is no committed card corpus (attack text is fetched from tcgdex at runtime), and the backlog names **no concrete SV card for count-2** while it names Cetoddle for the spread rider. A live tcgdex query CONFIRMED Cetoddle sv02-053 prints this text verbatim; count-2 remains a "family-completeness, dormant, no known consumer" relaxation. Per conventions ("prefer the first unless the code tells you otherwise"), the corpus reality — one candidate authors a real live card, the other zero verifiable cards — is exactly that signal, so this session took the spread rider. count-2 stays on the backlog, honestly labelled as dormant/unverified. **No web/API/DB/schema change** (a deriver-only slice; `spreadDamage` is engine-internal, surfaced via `DAMAGE_DEALT`). engineVersion 0.44.0 → **0.45.0**; 1958 → **1960** tests (138 files). `bun run check` green. **Self-reviewed, no independent reviewer** — a one-token relaxation of a just-verified regex. *Why: the two open candidates were equal in cost but not in value — verifying the actual catalog (rather than trusting the "cheapest first" label) showed one fires against a real card and the other against none, so "better and green" meant taking the one with yield.* (2026-07-27) |
| D95 | Umbreon "Feint Attack" — the any-target snipe that ignores Weakness/Resistance AND effects on the target (P3-M5, engine 0.46.0) | The resume point's first-named candidate, and the any-target snipe family's (D59) first rider that CHANGES the op rather than adding a card. Umbreon **sv03-130** (Obsidian Flames, in the live SV pool — tcgdex-confirmed this session, VERBATIM): "This attack does 50 damage to 1 of your opponent's Pokémon. This attack's damage isn't affected by Weakness or Resistance, or by any effects on that Pokémon." **A FIELD on the existing `damageChosen` op (`ignoreWR?: true`), not a new op** — threaded through `placeSnipe`/`snipeActive` as one boolean, mirroring the existing `deals` param. **The load-bearing discovery: the real SV text is LONGER than the backlog shorthand.** The backlog and prior resume points named this "Umbreon's no-W/R-on-Active flag" — an `ignoreWR` on the W/R math alone. But a live tcgdex query showed the printed sentence carries a SECOND clause, "…, or by any effects on that Pokémon.", which in the engine's snipe pipeline means the target's `damageReductionAfterWR` passive (Copperajah's Bronze Body, Bouffalant's Bouffer — the only target-side effect a snipe models). So the flag nulls Weakness, Resistance AND that reduction, on BOTH snipe arms (a Feint that lands on a benched reduction body skips it too). **What it KEEPS is the discriminating call:** the attacker's own pre-W/R bonus (`attackerPreWRBonus` — Vitality Band, Defiance Band, Choice Belt) STILL applies, because those are effects on the ATTACKER, not on "that Pokémon" (the target) — so a Feint + Vitality Band lands 50+10=60 FLAT (bonus added, not doubled inside a nulled Weakness). Modeling only the W/R half (matching the shorthand) would have DERIVED the card yet under-honored its own printed sentence against every reduction body — a latent bug — so the honest slice is the full sentence. **The deriver: one regex, not a twin.** `CHOSEN_ANY_TARGET`'s optional tail became an ALTERNATION — the existing Benched-clarifier parenthetical (no op change) OR a captured group for the Feint sentence (sets `ignoreWR`); the ", or by any effects on that Pokémon" clause is itself optional so the bare classic wording derives the same flag [🆕🆕🆕 D622 (D539): the same `ignoreWR`; the bare one ALSO sets `wrOnly` (W/R only, effects on the target apply — R-AM2 retired)]. A `toBeNull()` near-miss in `anyTargetSnipe.test.ts` (it listed Umbreon's short form as REFUSED "— a DIFFERENT tail") flipped to a positive derive — the D94 "a test moved, not just added" pattern. **Test-deck choice (the D91 reasoning, via a mid-slice course-correction):** the runtime tests first grew the shared `ANY_SNIPE_DECK`, which is a FIXED 60 — adding 6 cards overflowed it, and rebalancing the dominant `fix-lightning-weak` 30→24 reshuffled the seed-sensitive opening-hand pins and broke an EXISTING snipe test, so a dedicated mulligan-free `FEINT_DECK` was cut instead, leaving all 20+ existing snipe tests byte-identical. `board`/`anySnipe` gained an optional `deck` param (default unchanged). A synthetic {L} `fix-feint` (the card's VERBATIM SV text, but typed {L} so `fix-lightning-weak`'s Weakness fires and can be shown NULLED) is the runtime witness; the real card is pinned by the verbatim-text derive assertion. **No web/API/DB/schema change** (the snipe's damage is engine-internal, surfaced via `DAMAGE_DEALT`; `ignoreWR` just nulls two modifiers already in the event). engineVersion 0.45.0 → **0.46.0**; 1960 → **1964** tests (138 files). `bun run check` green. **Self-reviewed, no independent reviewer** — a one-field slice on the just-reviewed D59 snipe pipeline; two research sub-agents (a tcgdex verification + an Explore code-map) scoped it. *Why: the resume point named it first as a small "W/R flag"; verifying the actual print (rather than trusting the shorthand) turned it into a faithful "ignore all target-side modifiers" slice — the same D94 lesson that the catalog, not the backlog label, decides the real shape of the work.* (2026-07-27) |
| D96 | Chien-Pao ex "Hail Blade" — the variable-count discard whose damage scales off it, and the engine's FIRST registry-authored attack (P3-M5, engine 0.47.0) | The resume point's first-named candidate and the damage family's hardest card. Chien-Pao ex **sv02-061** (Paldea Evolved, live SV pool — tcgdex-confirmed VERBATIM this session): "Hail Blade" ({W}{W}, "60×") = "You may discard **any amount** of {W} Energy from **your Pokémon**. This attack does 60 damage for each card you discarded in this way." **The D95 lesson held again:** the real print is broader than the backlog shorthand ("discard from this Pokémon") — it is "from **your** Pokémon" (the whole own board) and "for each **card**" (count-scaled). **Three gaps, closed together, in one coherent slice, reusing the most existing machinery: Model A (post-damage, §9.2 record-driven).** §8 applies damage BEFORE the effect program, so an attack whose number depends on a mid-attack choice cannot use the pre-W/R `AttackDamageBonus` fold (read off the board at declaration) — the honest model is base 0 + a program op that re-enters the W/R math AFTER the discard. (1) `discardEnergy` gained a **`from: "yours"`** arm — the CONTROLLER's whole board, the printed "your Pokémon", distinct from `yourActive`'s "this Pokémon"; the candidate scan (`attachedEnergies` whole-board) and apply (`discardEnergyApply` strips active+bench) already supported it, so it was `discardVictimSeat` + a from-arm, not new enumeration. (2) A **`count: "any"`** mode — the one DECLINABLE discard (0..all), a new `DiscardScope {kind:"upTo", max}` whose pick MAY be empty (every other discard scope is mandatory/exact), never collapsed by interchangeability (the COUNT is what the damage reads, so N identical {W} must offer N rows) and never forced (a real decision at every non-empty board). (3) A §9.2 **`recordAs`** on `discardEnergy` filing the discarded uids under a NEW `discarded` `EffectSlot` — "for each card you discarded **in this way**" is literally an op→op record read (the Miriam/Superior-Energy-Retrieval shape), so `discardEnergyApply` now returns `{state, discarded}` and `recordMoved` fires at every discard site (a no-op for the 5 pre-existing discard cards, which set no `recordAs`). The reader is a **new `damageDefender` op** `{per, count: EffectSlot}` that deals `per × recorded(slot).length` to the DEFENDER through the EXISTING **`snipeActive`** — the same full §8.5 pipeline Umbreon/Wo-Chien use to hit the Active (Weakness DOUBLES, Resistance/reduction subtract, the attacker's continuous pre-W/R bonus ADDS) — so it is genuine attack damage, NOT the flat put-counter model. **Because the program owns the damage, the printed "60×" base is SUPPRESSED** (attack.ts: a `programDamage` check — a program containing `damageDefender` drops the base + marks the "×" modifier simulated, exactly as the multiply family drops its own base; otherwise a 0-discard Hail Blade would deal 60 flat + double-count, and the "×" would flag `ATTACK_EFFECT_SKIPPED`). **The FIRST registry-authored attack** (the `programFor(id)?.attack` seam, unused until now): CHIEN_PAO's existing Shivery-Chill entry gained the `attack` program, because the variable-count sentence is one the deriver (`deriveAttackEffect` + the multiply/additive readers) deliberately refuses — so `deriveAttackEffect(verbatim)` STILL returns null and the providesEnergy/scaledDamage near-miss tests stay green. **A client change too** (no schema/API/DB): since M9 the web app runs on the real engine, so `GameHud.tsx`'s discard dialog gained the upTo/declinable branch (a capped multi-select, "Discard none"/"Discard N", always-ready Confirm). **HONESTY NOTE — landed under a hard autoloop landing without its own behavioural tests.** engineVersion 0.46.0 → **0.47.0**; `bun run typecheck` green and the **engine suite unchanged-green (929 tests)** — proving the implementation compiles and breaks nothing existing — but the dedicated `hailBlade.test.ts` (damage scaling / decline-is-0 / whole-board / Weakness / ex-KO prizes) was being written by a sub-agent STOPPED at the hard-landing before it produced the file, so those behaviours are code-reviewed-by-eye only. **The resume point makes writing them the immediate next slice, then `/code-review`.** *Why: the resume point named it first and as "its own session"; the code-map confirmed a three-gap slice, but each gap reused an existing seam (whole-board apply, the snipe W/R pipeline, the §9.2 record), so the honest whole-sentence implementation was tractable in one pass — the missing piece is verification, deferred (not skipped) with a loud resume point.* **FOLLOW-UP (build #81, 2026-07-27) — the deferred verification LANDED and the honesty note is now CLOSED:** `packages/engine/src/hailBlade.test.ts` (10 tests) pins all seven named behaviours — registry-authored + deriver-still-refuses, the whole-board `upTo` park with no interchangeable collapse and no non-{W} offered, 60×k to the defender with the self-discard event and exact ordering, the DECLINE dealing zero (the base-suppression proof), a benched Pokémon's {W} as fuel while the Active's cost Energy stays on, Weakness DOUBLING the hit (the proof it re-enters §8.5 rather than putting flat counters), and an ex KO owing 2 Prizes (D93). Two fixtures added (`fix-water-weak`, `HAIL_BLADE_DECK`). **The implementation needed no behavioural fix** — the design above survived verification unchanged. **What DID surface: `bun run check` had been RED since this decision landed** — #80 ran only `typecheck` + the engine suite, so the lint gate never ran (`attack.ts` `useOptionalChain`, fixed behaviour-identically; a stale comment there denying any registry-authored attack corrected). *Lesson recorded: after a hard mid-flight landing, re-run the FULL gate, not the subset the interrupted session reached.* engineVersion unchanged at 0.47.0; 139 files / 1974 tests green. `/code-review` remains NOT run (user-triggered). (2026-07-27) |
| D97 | Mewtwo VSTAR "Psy Purge" — the CAPPED Hail Blade shape, and the authored-attack seam keyed by attack INDEX (P3-M5, engine 0.48.0) | The resume point's flagged "cheap adjacent option now that the machinery exists". Mewtwo VSTAR **swsh10.5-031** (Pokémon GO / Crown Zenith, live in the D1 catalog — tcgdex-confirmed VERBATIM, identical across all three prints -031/-079/-086): "Psy Purge" ({P}{C}, "90×") = "Discard up to 3 Psychic Energy from your Pokémon. This attack does 90 damage for each card you discarded in this way." — the SAME declinable whole-board discard → `damageDefender` D96 built, but the count is capped at 3 rather than "any amount". **Two small changes, both reusing D96's machinery.** **(1) The CAP — one optional field, not a new count mode.** `discardEnergy` gained **`cap?: number`**, meaningful only alongside `count: "any"`: it bounds the declinable up-to BELOW the whole board (the printed "up to N"), so the park's `{kind:"upTo", max}` becomes `min(cap, discardable.length)` instead of the full offer, and `discardNote` prints "up to 3 …" instead of "any amount of …". Rejected the alternative of a new `count` shape (`{upTo:N}`): the "any" branch already parks exactly right and the wire validator (`cardplay.ts` — "discard at most N Energy", `BAD_EFFECT_CHOICE`) was ALREADY cap-agnostic (it reads `scope.max`), so a narrower `max` needed zero wire change — the cap is purely a smaller ceiling on the existing declinable mode. **(2) The authored-attack seam is now keyed by ATTACK INDEX.** D96's `CardProgram.attack` was a single `EffectOp[]` bound to the card id — fine for single-attack Chien-Pao, but Mewtwo VSTAR has TWO attacks (Psy Purge idx 0, "Star Raid" the VSTAR-Power spread idx 1), and `programFor(id)?.attack` would have applied Psy Purge's discard-and-scale program to BOTH — Star Raid would wrongly park on a discard. So `attack` became **`{ readonly [attackIndex: number]: EffectOp[] }`** and `attack.ts` reads `programFor(id)?.attack?.[index]`: a card authors only the attacks whose sentence the deriver refuses; Star Raid (idx 1) finds no program, the deriver refuses its text, and it is honestly flagged `ATTACK_EFFECT_SKIPPED` — no discard, no damage. Chien-Pao migrated to `{ 0: [...] }` (its two pinned `hailBlade.test.ts` assertions updated to `?.attack?.[0]`); the only functional reader was `attack.ts:216`, so the blast radius was one line + the type + two test assertions. Binding by index (not by attack name) matches how the `attack` action already selects the attack. **A stale comment corrected, made reachable by this slice.** `cards.ts pokemonSuffixOf`'s doc claimed "The current SV-era catalog holds no V/VMAX/VSTAR at all, so Choice Belt's gate is dormant" — but the live catalog's Pokémon GO set (swsh10.5) IS an ingested prior-era set holding **13 Pokémon V, 2 VMAX, 5 VSTAR** (queried this session), so Choice Belt's V-gate is LIVE, and Mewtwo VSTAR is the first authored VSTAR. Comment-only fix (no behaviour). **Tests:** `packages/engine/src/psyPurge.test.ts` (11 tests) — authored-at-idx-0-with-cap-3 + idx-1-undefined + all three prints; the CAP (park max 3 not 5, "up to 3" caption, a 4-pick rejected by the validator); 90×k scaling (3→270 survives a 280 HP Mewtwo defender, 1→90, decline→0); **Star Raid idx 1 stays unsimulated** (the index-keying proof — no park, no damage, `ATTACK_EFFECT_SKIPPED`); Weakness DOUBLING (90×2=180 vs a ×2 Psychic body); and a VSTAR KO owing **2 Prizes** (D93 — the first VSTAR the authored pool KOs). New fixtures `fix-psychic-weak`, the `swsh10.5-031` Mewtwo VSTAR (both attacks, VERBATIM), and `PSY_PURGE_DECK` (mirrors `HAIL_BLADE_DECK`, {P}-flavoured). **Every test passed on first run — no behavioural fix.** No web/API/DB/schema change (swsh10.5 already ingested; the HUD's existing upTo dialog carries the cap via the smaller `scope.max`). engineVersion 0.47.0 → **0.48.0**; 139 → **140** files, 1974 → **1985** tests. Full `bun run check` GREEN (tsc + lint + vitest — the D96 lesson: ran the FULL gate, twice, including after the comment edit). **Self-reviewed, no independent reviewer** — a contained slice extending D96's just-verified machinery; one Explore sub-agent mapped the seam and verified the catalog, and the exact print was re-confirmed against the live D1 before authoring. `/code-review` still NOT run (user-triggered; D96 + this slice both await it). *Why: the resume point flagged it cheap because the discard/record/damageDefender machinery already existed — the real work was the two seams the shorthand hid (a cap on the up-to, and index-keying so a multi-attack card doesn't misfire), each a small honest change, and the D95/D96 "verify the verbatim print" lesson held a third time (the text matched the shorthand here, but the catalog query is what confirmed the card, its ids, and the stale V/VSTAR comment).* (2026-07-27) |
| D98 | `damageAttacker` — the reactive counters-on-attacker recoil, landed as a defender-side PASSIVE rather than the `onDamagedByAttack` trigger the backlog named (P3-M5, engine 0.49.0) | The resume point named `onDamagedByAttack` first (4 cards) with `damageAttacker` (3 cards) as "the natural pair", each a "full new-op slice … scope for a whole session". **Two research sub-agents flipped the framing** (the D95/D96/D97 "verify before scoping" lesson, applied to the ARCHITECTURE, not just the text): a verbatim D1 query + an Explore code-map showed the `damageAttacker` subset (Cacnea/Cacturne "Counterattack Quills" 30, Stunfisk "Custom Trap" 50) is NOT a trigger at all — "if this Pokémon is damaged by an attack, put N damage counters on the Attacking Pokémon" is a **reactive continuous read, the exact shape of `damageReductionAfterWR`** (also read at damage time, seat-free).
